\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 01\quad\small\texttt{(d1-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 01 (d1-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) is given by \( y = f(x) \) where \( f(x) = x^2 \).\\[3pt]  Find the gradient of the chord joining the point where \( x = 2 \) to the point where:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 3 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = 2.5 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = 2.1 \)\\[3pt]  What does this suggest about the gradient of the curve at \( x = 2 \)?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recall: gradient of chord from \( x = 2 \) to \( x = a \) is \( \dfrac{f(a) - f(2)}{a - 2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{a^2 - 4}{a - 2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(2) = 4 \).

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{9 - 4}{3 - 2} = \frac{5}{1} = 5
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(3) = 9 \).

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( x = 2.5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{6.25 - 4}{2.5 - 2} = \frac{2.25}{0.5} = 4.5
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(2.5) = 6.25 \).

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( x = 2.1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{4.41 - 4}{2.1 - 2} = \frac{0.41}{0.1} = 4.1
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(2.1) = 4.41 \).

\textit{Step 5: As the second point gets closer to \( x = 2 \), the chord gradient approaches 4.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient at } x = 2 \approx 4
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient of the curve at \( x = 2 \) is 4. The diagram shows the three chords closing in on the tangent at \( P(2,4) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) 5 \quad  (ii) 4.5 \quad  (iii) 4.1 \quad  The chord gradients approach 4, suggesting the gradient of the curve at \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \) is 4.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 02\quad\small\texttt{(d1-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 02 (d1-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) is given by \( y = x^2 \). Find the gradient of the chord joining the point \( (3, 9) \) to the point where:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 4 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = 3.5 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = 3.01 \)\\[3pt]  Hence state the gradient of the curve at \( x = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Chord gradient formula: \( \dfrac{f(a) - f(3)}{a - 3} = \dfrac{a^2 - 9}{a - 3} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{a^2 - 9}{a - 3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(3) = 9 \).

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{16 - 9}{4 - 3} = 7
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(4) = 16 \).

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( x = 3.5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{12.25 - 9}{3.5 - 3} = \frac{3.25}{0.5} = 6.5
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(3.5) = 12.25 \).

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( x = 3.01 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{9.0601 - 9}{3.01 - 3} = \frac{0.0601}{0.01} = 6.01
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(3.01) = 9.0601 \).

\textit{Step 5: The values approach 6.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient at } x = 3 = 6
\end{gathered}\]
As the second point approaches \( x = 3 \), the chord gradient approaches 6.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) 7 \quad  (ii) 6.5 \quad  (iii) 6.01 \quad  Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 3 \) is 6.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 03\quad\small\texttt{(d1-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 03 (d1-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) is given by \( f(x) = x^3 \). Find the gradient of the chord from \( (2, 8) \) to the point where:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 3 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = 2.5 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = 2.1 \)\\[3pt]  What value does the gradient appear to be approaching?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Chord gradient: \( \dfrac{a^3 - 8}{a - 2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{a^3 - 8}{a - 2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(2) = 8 \).

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{27 - 8}{3 - 2} = 19
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3^3 = 27 \).

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( x = 2.5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{15.625 - 8}{2.5 - 2} = \frac{7.625}{0.5} = 15.25
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2.5^3 = 15.625 \).

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( x = 2.1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{9.261 - 8}{2.1 - 2} = \frac{1.261}{0.1} = 12.61
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2.1^3 = 9.261 \).

\textit{Step 5: The values approach 12.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient at } x = 2 \approx 12
\end{gathered}\]
This matches \( 3x^2 \big|_{x=2} = 12 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (i) 19 \quad (ii) 15.25 \quad (iii) 12.61 \quad The gradient appears to approach 12.\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 04\quad\small\texttt{(d1-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 04 (d1-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) is given by \( f(x) = x^2 + 3x \). Find the gradient of the chord joining the point where \( x = 1 \) to the point where:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 2 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = 1.5 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = 1.01 \)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Calculate \( f(1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(1) = 1 + 3 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Starting point is \( (1, 4) \).

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( f(2) = 4 + 6 = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{10 - 4}{2 - 1} = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( f(1.5) = 2.25 + 4.5 = 6.75 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{6.75 - 4}{1.5 - 1} = \frac{2.75}{0.5} = 5.5
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( f(1.01) = 1.0201 + 3.03 = 4.0501 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{4.0501 - 4}{1.01 - 1} = \frac{0.0501}{0.01} = 5.01
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient approaching 5.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) 6 \quad  (ii) 5.5 \quad  (iii) 5.01 \quad  The gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \) appears to be 5.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 05\quad\small\texttt{(d1-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 05 (d1-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The gradient of the curve \( y = x^2 \) at the point \( (4, 16) \) is stated to be 8.\\[3pt]  Verify this by finding the gradient of chords from \( x = 4 \) to \( x = 5 \), \( x = 4.5 \), and \( x = 4.1 \), and commenting on the pattern.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( x = 5 \): \( f(5) = 25 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{25 - 16}{5 - 4} = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 2: \( x = 4.5 \): \( f(4.5) = 20.25 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{20.25 - 16}{4.5 - 4} = \frac{4.25}{0.5} = 8.5
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 3: \( x = 4.1 \): \( f(4.1) = 16.81 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{16.81 - 16}{4.1 - 4} = \frac{0.81}{0.1} = 8.1
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 4: Pattern: the chord gradients approach 8 as the second point approaches \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
9 \to 8.5 \to 8.1 \to 8
\end{gathered}\]
This confirms the gradient at \( x = 4 \) is 8.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Chord gradients are 9, 8.5, 8.1 --- these approach 8, confirming the gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \) is 8.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 06\quad\small\texttt{(d1-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 06 (d1-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 \) from first principles to show that \( f'(x) = 2x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write the first principles formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
This is the definition of the derivative.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( f(x) = x^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{(x+h)^2 - x^2}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( f(x+h) \) with \( (x+h)^2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Expand \( (x+h)^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{x^2 + 2xh + h^2 - x^2}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
\( (x+h)^2 = x^2 + 2xh + h^2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Simplify the numerator.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{2xh + h^2}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} (2x + h)
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( h \) from numerator and denominator (valid since \( h \neq 0 \)).

\textit{Step 5: Take the limit as \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 2x
\end{gathered}\]
As \( h \to 0 \), \( 2x + h \to 2x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2x \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 07\quad\small\texttt{(d1-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 07 (d1-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^3 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write the first principles formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{(x+h)^3 - x^3}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Definition of the derivative with \( f(x) = x^3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand \( (x+h)^3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+h)^3 = x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Using the binomial expansion or repeated multiplication.

\textit{Step 3: Simplify the numerator.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3 - x^3}{h} = \frac{3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x^3 \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 4: Cancel \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 3x^2 + 3xh + h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Divide every term by \( h \).

\textit{Step 5: Take the limit as \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
The terms involving \( h \) vanish.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 3x^2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 08\quad\small\texttt{(d1-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 08 (d1-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = 3x^2 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the first principles formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{3(x+h)^2 - 3x^2}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(x+h) = 3(x+h)^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand and simplify the numerator.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{3(x^2 + 2xh + h^2) - 3x^2}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{6xh + 3h^2}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the bracket then cancel \( 3x^2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Cancel \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= \lim_{h \to 0} (6x + 3h) = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
As \( h \to 0 \), the \( 3h \) term vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 6x \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 09\quad\small\texttt{(d1-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 09 (d1-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = 2x^2 + 5 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = 2(x+h)^2 + 5 = 2x^2 + 4xh + 2h^2 + 5
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the bracket.

\textit{Step 2: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{(2x^2 + 4xh + 2h^2 + 5) - (2x^2 + 5)}{h} = \frac{4xh + 2h^2}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 2x^2 \) and \( 5 \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Simplify and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 4x + 2h \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 4x
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( h \), then let \( h \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 4x \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 10\quad\small\texttt{(d1-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 10 (d1-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Differentiate \( y = 5x + 3 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = 5(x+h) + 3 = 5x + 5h + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x + h \) for \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{(5x + 5h + 3) - (5x + 3)}{h} = \frac{5h}{h} = 5
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 5x \) and \( 3 \) terms cancel, leaving just \( 5 \).

\textit{Step 3: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\lim_{h \to 0} 5 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
The result is already constant --- the gradient of a straight line is constant, as expected.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 5 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 11\quad\small\texttt{(d1-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 11 (d1-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 - x \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = (x+h)^2 - (x+h) = x^2 + 2xh + h^2 - x - h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand each term.

\textit{Step 2: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{(x^2 + 2xh + h^2 - x - h) - (x^2 - x)}{h} = \frac{2xh + h^2 - h}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x^2 \) and \( -x \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Cancel \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 2x + h - 1 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 2x - 1
\end{gathered}\]
The \( h \) term vanishes as \( h \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2x - 1 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 12\quad\small\texttt{(d1-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 12 (d1-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = 2x^2 + 3x \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = 2(x+h)^2 + 3(x+h) = 2x^2 + 4xh + 2h^2 + 3x + 3h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( 2(x+h)^2 \) and \( 3(x+h) \) separately.

\textit{Step 2: Subtract \( f(x) = 2x^2 + 3x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) - f(x) = 4xh + 2h^2 + 3h
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 2x^2 \) and \( 3x \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{4xh + 2h^2 + 3h}{h} = 4x + 2h + 3 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 4x + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( h \), then let \( h \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 4x + 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 13\quad\small\texttt{(d1-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 13 (d1-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Differentiate \( y = 3x^2 - 4x + 1 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = 3(x+h)^2 - 4(x+h) + 1 = 3x^2 + 6xh + 3h^2 - 4x - 4h + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Expand each term carefully.

\textit{Step 2: Subtract \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 4x + 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) - f(x) = 6xh + 3h^2 - 4h
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 3x^2 \), \( -4x \) and \( +1 \) terms all cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{6xh + 3h^2 - 4h}{h} = 6x + 3h - 4
\end{gathered}\]
Divide every term by \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\lim_{h \to 0}(6x + 3h - 4) = 6x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 3h \) term vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x - 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 14\quad\small\texttt{(d1-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 14 (d1-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 + 4x - 7 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = (x+h)^2 + 4(x+h) - 7 = x^2 + 2xh + h^2 + 4x + 4h - 7
\end{gathered}\]
Expand all brackets.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 2xh + h^2 + 4h
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x^2 \), \( 4x \) and \( -7 \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 2x + h + 4 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 2x + 4
\end{gathered}\]
The \( h \) term vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2x + 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 15\quad\small\texttt{(d1-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 15 (d1-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{By differentiating \( f(x) = x^2 \) from first principles, find the gradient of the curve at the point \( (5, 25) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 \) from first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{(x+h)^2 - x^2}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{2xh + h^2}{h} = 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Standard first-principles derivation.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( x = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(5) = 2 \times 5 = 10
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( x \) with 5 in the derivative. The diagram shows the tangent with gradient 10 touching the curve at \( (5, 25) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  (5, 25) \) is \(\displaystyle  10 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 16\quad\small\texttt{(d1-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 16 (d1-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{By differentiating \( f(x) = x^3 \) from first principles, find the gradient of the curve at \( x = -2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate from first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{(x+h)^3 - x^3}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Definition of derivative.

\textit{Step 2: Expand \( (x+h)^3 \) and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0}(3x^2 + 3xh + h^2) = 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( h \) and take the limit.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = -2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(-2) = 3(-2)^2 = 3 \times 4 = 12
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient is positive here.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = -2 \) is \(\displaystyle  12 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 17\quad\small\texttt{(d1-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 17 (d1-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{By differentiating \( f(x) = 2x^2 \) from first principles, find the gradient of the tangent to the curve at the point \( (-3, 18) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate from first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{2(x+h)^2 - 2x^2}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{4xh + 2h^2}{h} = 4x
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( h \) and take the limit.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate at \( x = -3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(-3) = 4(-3) = -12
\end{gathered}\]
The negative gradient means the curve is decreasing at this point.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient of the tangent at \(\displaystyle  (-3, 18) \) is \(\displaystyle  -12 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 18\quad\small\texttt{(d1-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 18 (d1-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{By differentiating from first principles, find the gradient of \( y = x^2 - 3x \) at the point where \( x = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
[(x+h)^2 - 3(x+h)] - [x^2 - 3x] = 2xh + h^2 - 3h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and cancel.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + h - 3 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 2x - 3
\end{gathered}\]
So \( f'(x) = 2x - 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(4) = 2(4) - 3 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate the derivative at the given point.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \) is \(\displaystyle  5 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 19\quad\small\texttt{(d1-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 19 (d1-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 4x^2 - 1 \). By differentiating from first principles, find the gradient at the point \( (2, 15) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
[4(x+h)^2 - 1] - [4x^2 - 1] = 4(x^2 + 2xh + h^2) - 4x^2 = 8xh + 4h^2
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 4x^2 \) and \( -1 \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
8x + 4h \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 8x
\end{gathered}\]
So \( f'(x) = 8x \).

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(2) = 8 \times 2 = 16
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient at \( (2, 15) \) is 16.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  (2, 15) \) is \(\displaystyle  16 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 20\quad\small\texttt{(d1-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 20 (d1-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 + 6 \).\\[3pt]  (a) Differentiate from first principles to find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \).\\[3pt]  (b) Find the coordinates of the point on \( C \) where the gradient is 8.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Apply first principles to \( y = x^2 + 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h} = \frac{(x+h)^2 + 6 - x^2 - 6}{h} = \frac{2xh + h^2}{h} = 2x + h \xrightarrow{h\to 0} 2x
\end{gathered}\]
The constant 6 cancels out.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 8 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = 8 \Rightarrow x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( y \)-coordinate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4^2 + 6 = 22
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 4 \) into the original equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  (4, 22) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 21\quad\small\texttt{(d1-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 21 (d1-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 3x^2 \).\\[3pt]  (a) Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \) by differentiating from first principles.\\[3pt]  (b) Find the coordinates of the point on \( C \) where the gradient is \( -18 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) From first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{3(x+h)^2 - 3x^2}{h} = \frac{6xh + 3h^2}{h} = 6x + 3h \xrightarrow{h\to 0} 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Standard calculation.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( 6x = -18 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 6.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3(-3)^2 = 27
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = -3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  (-3, 27) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 22\quad\small\texttt{(d1-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 22 (d1-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 - 5x \).\\[3pt]  (a) Differentiate from first principles to find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \).\\[3pt]  (b) Find the \( x \)-value where the tangent to \( C \) is parallel to the \( x \)-axis.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) From first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) - f(x) = [(x+h)^2 - 5(x+h)] - [x^2 - 5x] = 2xh + h^2 - 5h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and simplify.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + h - 5 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 2x - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Derivative is \( 2x - 5 \).

\textit{Step 3: (b) A tangent parallel to the \( x \)-axis has gradient 0.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 5 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 2.5
\end{gathered}\]
Solve \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x - 5 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = 2.5 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 23\quad\small\texttt{(d1-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 23 (d1-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 \).\\[3pt]  (a) Differentiate from first principles to show \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 \).\\[3pt]  (b) Find the two values of \( x \) where the gradient of \( C \) is 27.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) From first principles (see d1-007 for full working).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Standard first-principles result for \( y = x^3 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( 3x^2 = 27 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 9 \Rightarrow x = \pm 3
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 3 then square-root, giving two solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle  x = 3 \) or \(\displaystyle  x = -3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 24\quad\small\texttt{(d1-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 24 (d1-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 + 3 \). A tangent to \( C \) has gradient 10.\\[3pt]  (a) Differentiate \( y = x^2 + 3 \) from first principles.\\[3pt]  (b) Find the equation of the tangent.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) From first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(x+h)^2 + 3 - x^2 - 3}{h} = \frac{2xh + h^2}{h} \xrightarrow{h\to0} 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Constant 3 cancels out.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( 2x = 10 \) to find the point of tangency.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 5 \Rightarrow y = 25 + 3 = 28
\end{gathered}\]
Point of tangency is \( (5, 28) \).

\textit{Step 3: Use \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y - 28 = 10(x - 5) \Rightarrow y = 10x - 22
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient \( m = 10 \), point \( (5, 28) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  y = 10x - 22 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 25\quad\small\texttt{(d1-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 25 (d1-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Explain what \( f'(x) \) represents geometrically for the curve \( y = f(x) \), and describe what it means when \( f'(x) > 0 \), \( f'(x) = 0 \), and \( f'(x) < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( f'(x) \) is the gradient of the curve at the point \( (x, f(x)) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \text{gradient of tangent at } x
\end{gathered}\]
It measures the instantaneous rate of change of \( y \) with respect to \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: \( f'(x) > 0 \): the curve is increasing (sloping upward) at that point.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) > 0 \Rightarrow \text{curve is increasing}
\end{gathered}\]
Moving left to right, \( y \) is getting larger.

\textit{Step 3: \( f'(x) = 0 \): the tangent is horizontal --- the curve has a stationary point.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow \text{stationary point (turning point or point of inflection)}
\end{gathered}\]
The curve is momentarily neither increasing nor decreasing.

\textit{Step 4: \( f'(x) < 0 \): the curve is decreasing at that point.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) < 0 \Rightarrow \text{curve is decreasing}
\end{gathered}\]
Moving left to right, \( y \) is getting smaller.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) \) is the gradient of the tangent. Positive: increasing; zero: stationary; negative: decreasing.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 26\quad\small\texttt{(d1-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 26 (d1-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The function \( f(x) = x^2 - 4x + 1 \) has derivative \( f'(x) = 2x - 4 \).\\[3pt]  (a) Find the value of \( x \) where the gradient is zero.\\[3pt]  (b) Determine whether the function is increasing or decreasing at \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Set \( f'(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 4 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The curve has a stationary point at \( x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) At \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(0) = 2(0) - 4 = -4 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
The function is decreasing at \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: At \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(4) = 2(4) - 4 = 4 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
The function is increasing at \( x = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \) \quad  (b) Decreasing at \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \); increasing at \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 27\quad\small\texttt{(d1-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 27 (d1-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Explain the difference between the gradient of a chord and the gradient of a tangent to a curve at a point, and how differentiation from first principles connects the two.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: A chord is a straight line joining two distinct points on a curve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient of chord} = \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
This is an average rate of change over an interval of width \( h \).

\textit{Step 2: A tangent touches the curve at exactly one point, giving the instantaneous rate of change.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient of tangent} = f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
As the two points on the chord are brought infinitely close together (\( h \to 0 \)), the chord becomes the tangent.

\textit{Step 3: First principles shows that the tangent gradient is the limit of the chord gradients.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Chord gradient} \xrightarrow{h \to 0} \text{Tangent gradient}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiating from first principles formalises this limiting process.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad A chord connects two points (average rate of change); a tangent touches at one point (instantaneous rate). First principles takes the limit as the chord length shrinks to zero, turning the chord gradient into the tangent gradient.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 28\quad\small\texttt{(d1-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 28 (d1-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The area \( A \) cm\textsuperscript{2} of a square with side \( x \) cm is given by \( A = x^2 \).\\[3pt]  By differentiating from first principles, find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}A}{\mathrm{d}x} \) and interpret its meaning.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply first principles to \( A = x^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(x+h)^2 - x^2}{h} = \frac{2xh + h^2}{h} = 2x + h \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Standard first-principles derivation.

\textit{Step 2: Interpret the result.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}A}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}A}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x \) represents the rate at which the area increases as the side length increases. At any side length \( x \), the area increases by approximately \( 2x \) cm\textsuperscript{2} for each additional cm of side length.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}A}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x \). It represents the rate of increase of area per unit increase in side length --- numerically equal to the perimeter of two sides.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 29\quad\small\texttt{(d1-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 29 (d1-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Show, by differentiating from first principles, that the derivative of any constant \( f(x) = c \) is zero.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write the first principles formula for \( f(x) = c \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{c - c}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( f(x) = c \) for all \( x \), \( f(x+h) = c \) as well.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{0}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} 0 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Zero divided by anything non-zero is zero.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 0 \) for any constant \(\displaystyle  f(x) = c \). This makes sense --- a horizontal line has zero gradient.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 30\quad\small\texttt{(d1-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 30 (d1-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x \) from first principles and comment on the result.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write the first principles formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{(x+h) - x}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{h}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} 1 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
The \( x \) terms cancel, leaving \( h/h = 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Comment.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 1 \text{ for all } x
\end{gathered}\]
This is expected: \( y = x \) is a straight line with gradient 1 everywhere.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 1 \). The gradient of \(\displaystyle  y = x \) is constant at 1 for all \(\displaystyle  x \), which matches the slope of the line.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 31\quad\small\texttt{(d1-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 31 (d1-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{(a) Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 + 2x - 3 \) from first principles.\\[3pt]  (b) Hence find the gradient of the curve at the point where \( x = -1 \).\\[3pt]  (c) Find the equation of the tangent to the curve at this point.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Find \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
[(x+h)^2 + 2(x+h) - 3] - [x^2 + 2x - 3] = 2xh + h^2 + 2h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and cancel terms.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + h + 2 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 2x + 2
\end{gathered}\]
So \( f'(x) = 2x + 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: (b) Substitute \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(-1) = 2(-1) + 2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient at \( x = -1 \) is 0 --- this is a stationary point.

\textit{Step 4: (c) Find the \( y \)-coordinate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(-1) = 1 - 2 - 3 = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Point of tangency is \( (-1, -4) \).

\textit{Step 5: Write the tangent equation (horizontal line since gradient = 0).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -4
\end{gathered}\]
A tangent with gradient 0 at \( (-1, -4) \) is the horizontal line \( y = -4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2x + 2 \) \quad  (b) Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 0 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  y = -4 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 32\quad\small\texttt{(d1-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 32 (d1-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{(a) Show, by differentiating \( f(x) = x^2 \) from first principles, that \( f'(x) = 2x \).\\[3pt]  (b) The point \( P \) lies on the curve \( y = x^2 \). The gradient of the curve at \( P \) is \(-6\). Find the coordinates of \( P \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) From first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(x+h)^2 - x^2}{h} = \frac{2xh + h^2}{h} = 2x + h \xrightarrow{h\to 0} 2x \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
This confirms \( f'(x) = 2x \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( 2x = -6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = (-3)^2 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Coordinates of \( P \) are \( (-3, 9) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle  P = (-3, 9) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 33\quad\small\texttt{(d1-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 33 (d1-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 2x^2 - x \).\\[3pt]  (a) Differentiate from first principles to find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \).\\[3pt]  (b) Find the gradient of \( C \) at the point \( A(2, 6) \).\\[3pt]  (c) Find the equation of the tangent to \( C \) at \( A \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Find \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
[2(x+h)^2 - (x+h)] - [2x^2 - x] = 4xh + 2h^2 - h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and cancel \( 2x^2 \) and \( -x \) terms.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x + 2h - 1 \xrightarrow{h \to 0} 4x - 1
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x - 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: (b) Substitute \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4(2) - 1 = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient at \( A \) is 7.

\textit{Step 4: (c) Equation of tangent using \( y - 6 = 7(x - 2) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 7x - 14 + 6 = 7x - 8
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( m = 7 \) and point \( (2, 6) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x - 1 \) \quad  (b) Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 7 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  y = 7x - 8 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 34\quad\small\texttt{(d1-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 34 (d1-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 2 \).\\[3pt]  (a) By differentiating from first principles, show that \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 \).\\[3pt]  (b) Find the two points on \( C \) where the gradient is 12.\\[3pt]  (c) Find the gradient of \( C \) at the origin.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate \( y = x^3 - 2 \) from first principles.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(x+h)^3 - 2 - (x^3 - 2)}{h} = \frac{3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3}{h} = 3x^2 + 3xh + h^2 \xrightarrow{h\to 0} 3x^2 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
The \( -2 \) terms cancel; the rest follows from expanding \( (x+h)^3 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( 3x^2 = 12 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 4 \Rightarrow x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Two values of \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find corresponding \( y \)-values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2: y = 8 - 2 = 6 \qquad x = -2: y = -8 - 2 = -10
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) into \( y = x^3 - 2 \).

\textit{Step 4: (c) Gradient at origin (\( x = 0 \)).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x}\bigg|_{x=0} = 3(0)^2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve has a stationary point at the origin.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle  (2, 6) \) and \(\displaystyle  (-2, -10) \) \quad  (c) Gradient at origin is 0.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 35\quad\small\texttt{(d1-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 35 (d1-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{(a) Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 \) from first principles to show that \( f'(x) = 2x \).\\[3pt]  (b) The tangent to \( y = x^2 \) at the point \( P(a, a^2) \) has equation \( y = 2ax - a^2 \). Verify this using the result from part (a).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) From first principles (standard).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h\to 0}\frac{(x+h)^2 - x^2}{h} = \lim_{h\to 0}(2x+h) = 2x \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Standard first-principles derivation.

\textit{Step 2: (b) At \( x = a \), the gradient is \( f'(a) = 2a \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient} = 2a
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = a \) into the derivative.

\textit{Step 3: Use \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \) with \( m = 2a \) and point \( (a, a^2) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y - a^2 = 2a(x - a)
\end{gathered}\]
Point-gradient form of the line.

\textit{Step 4: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2ax - 2a^2 + a^2 = 2ax - a^2 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
This matches the given tangent equation, confirming the result.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Verified: tangent at \(\displaystyle  P(a, a^2) \) is \(\displaystyle  y = 2ax - a^2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 36\quad\small\texttt{(d1-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 36 (d1-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 + x \). Find the gradient of the chord from the point where \( x = 1 \) to the point where:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 2 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = 1.1 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = 1.01 \)\\[3pt]  Hence suggest the gradient of \( C \) at \( x = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = x^2 + x \). Then \( f(1) = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Chord gradient} = \frac{f(a) - 2}{a - 1}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the chord-gradient formula.

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( a = 2 \), \( f(2) = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{6 - 2}{2 - 1} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient from \( x = 1 \) to \( x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( a = 1.1 \), \( f(1.1) = 2.31 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2.31 - 2}{1.1 - 1} = \frac{0.31}{0.1} = 3.1
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient closer to \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( a = 1.01 \), \( f(1.01) = 2.0301 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2.0301 - 2}{0.01} = 3.01
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient very close to \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 5: The chord gradients approach 3.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient at } x = 1 \approx 3
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( f'(x) = 2x + 1 \), \( f'(1) = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) 4 \quad  (ii) 3.1 \quad  (iii) 3.01 \quad  Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \) is 3.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 37\quad\small\texttt{(d1-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 37 (d1-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The curve \( C \) is given by \( y = x^3 - x \). Find the gradient of the chord from the point \( (1, 0) \) to the point where:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 1.5 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = 1.1 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = 1.01 \)\\[3pt]  What does this suggest about the gradient of \( C \) at \( x = 1 \)?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = x^3 - x \), so \( f(1) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Chord gradient} = \frac{f(a) - 0}{a - 1}
\end{gathered}\]
Chord from \( (1, 0) \) to \( (a, f(a)) \).

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( a = 1.5 \), \( f(1.5) = 1.875 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1.875}{0.5} = 3.75
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( a = 1.1 \), \( f(1.1) = 0.231 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{0.231}{0.1} = 2.31
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( a = 1.01 \), \( f(1.01) = 0.020301 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{0.020301}{0.01} = 2.0301
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 5: The chord gradients approach 2.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient at } x = 1 \approx 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( f'(x) = 3x^2 - 1 \), so \( f'(1) = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) 3.75 \quad  (ii) 2.31 \quad  (iii) 2.0301 \quad  Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \) is 2.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 38\quad\small\texttt{(d1-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 38 (d1-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{For the curve \( y = 2x^2 \), find the gradient of the chord joining \( x = -1 \) to:\\[3pt]  (i) \( x = 0 \) \quad  (ii) \( x = -0.5 \) \quad  (iii) \( x = -0.9 \)\\[3pt]  Deduce the gradient of the curve at \( x = -1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = 2x^2 \), \( f(-1) = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Chord gradient} = \frac{f(a) - 2}{a - (-1)} = \frac{2a^2 - 2}{a + 1}
\end{gathered}\]
Chord-gradient formula.

\textit{Step 2: (i) \( a = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{0 - 2}{0 + 1} = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 3: (ii) \( a = -0.5 \), \( f(-0.5) = 0.5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{0.5 - 2}{0.5} = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 4: (iii) \( a = -0.9 \), \( f(-0.9) = 1.62 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1.62 - 2}{0.1} = -3.8
\end{gathered}\]
Chord gradient.

\textit{Step 5: The chord gradients approach \( -4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 4x \Rightarrow f'(-1) = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Consistent with differentiation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  -2 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  -3 \) \quad  (iii) \(\displaystyle  -3.8 \) \quad  Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = -1 \) is \(\displaystyle  -4 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 39\quad\small\texttt{(d1-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 39 (d1-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A student estimates the gradient of \( y = x^2 \) at \( x = 4 \) using chords to \( x = 4 + h \) for \( h = 1, 0.1, 0.01 \). Tabulate the three chord gradients and state what the gradient at \( x = 4 \) appears to be.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Chord gradient \( = \dfrac{(4+h)^2 - 16}{h} = 8 + h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(4+h)^2 - 16}{h} = \frac{8h + h^2}{h} = 8 + h
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify using algebra.

\textit{Step 2: \( h = 1 \Rightarrow \) gradient \( = 9 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
8 + 1 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( h = 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: \( h = 0.1 \Rightarrow \) gradient \( = 8.1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
8 + 0.1 = 8.1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( h = 0.1 \).

\textit{Step 4: \( h = 0.01 \Rightarrow \) gradient \( = 8.01 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
8 + 0.01 = 8.01
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( h = 0.01 \).

\textit{Step 5: As \( h \to 0 \), chord gradient \( \to 8 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Gradient at } x = 4 = 8
\end{gathered}\]
The limit is 8. The diagram shows how the chords flatten onto the tangent at \( (4, 16) \) as \( h \) shrinks.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Chord gradients: 9, 8.1, 8.01. Gradient of curve at \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \) is 8.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 40\quad\small\texttt{(d1-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 40 (d1-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 3 - x^2 \). Find the gradient of the chord joining \( (2, -1) \) to the point where \( x = 2 + h \). Simplify your answer and deduce the gradient of \( C \) at \( x = 2 \) by letting \( h \to 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute \( f(2+h) \) where \( f(x) = 3 - x^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(2+h) = 3 - (2+h)^2 = 3 - 4 - 4h - h^2 = -1 - 4h - h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( (2+h)^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Chord gradient \( = \dfrac{f(2+h) - f(2)}{h} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(-1 - 4h - h^2) - (-1)}{h} = \frac{-4h - h^2}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( f(2) = -1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= -4 - h
\end{gathered}\]
Divide every term by \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Let \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\lim_{h \to 0}(-4 - h) = -4
\end{gathered}\]
The chord gradient tends to \( -4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Chord gradient \(\displaystyle  = -4 - h \). Gradient of \(\displaystyle  C \) at \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \) is \(\displaystyle  -4 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 41\quad\small\texttt{(d1-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 41 (d1-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = 3x^2 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write down the definition of the derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard first-principles definition.

\textit{Step 2: Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = 3(x+h)^2 = 3x^2 + 6xh + 3h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( (x+h)^2 \) and multiply by 3.

\textit{Step 3: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{3x^2 + 6xh + 3h^2 - 3x^2}{h} = \frac{6xh + 3h^2}{h} = 6x + 3h
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract, then divide by \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}(6x + 3h) = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 3h \) term vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 6x \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 42\quad\small\texttt{(d1-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 42 (d1-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 - 4x \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Definition of derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard definition.

\textit{Step 2: Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = (x+h)^2 - 4(x+h) = x^2 + 2xh + h^2 - 4x - 4h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand brackets.

\textit{Step 3: Form difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2xh + h^2 - 4h}{h} = 2x + h - 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^2 \) and \( -4x \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit as \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 2x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
The \( h \) term vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2x - 4 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 43\quad\small\texttt{(d1-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 43 (d1-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Use first principles to differentiate \( f(x) = x^2 + 3x - 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = (x+h)^2 + 3(x+h) - 1 = x^2 + 2xh + h^2 + 3x + 3h - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Expand all terms.

\textit{Step 2: Compute \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2xh + h^2 + 3h
\end{gathered}\]
Constant and \( x \) terms cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2xh + h^2 + 3h}{h} = 2x + h + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify.

\textit{Step 4: Let \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 2x + 3
\end{gathered}\]
The \( -1 \) constant has no effect on the derivative.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2x + 3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 44\quad\small\texttt{(d1-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 44 (d1-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = 2x^3 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand \( (x+h)^3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+h)^3 = x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Binomial expansion.

\textit{Step 2: Compute \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2(x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3) - 2x^3 = 6x^2h + 6xh^2 + 2h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract and collect terms.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{6x^2h + 6xh^2 + 2h^3}{h} = 6x^2 + 6xh + 2h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel one factor of \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}(6x^2 + 6xh + 2h^2) = 6x^2
\end{gathered}\]
All \( h \) terms vanish.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 6x^2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 45\quad\small\texttt{(d1-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 45 (d1-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+h)^3 + 2(x+h) = x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3 + 2x + 2h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand cube and linear term.

\textit{Step 2: Subtract \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3 + 2h
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^3 \) and \( 2x \) cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}(3x^2 + 3xh + h^2 + 2) = 3x^2 + 2
\end{gathered}\]
All \( h \) terms vanish.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 3x^2 + 2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 46\quad\small\texttt{(d1-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 46 (d1-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Use first principles to show that the derivative of \( f(x) = 5x^2 - 3 \) is \( 10x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x+h) = 5(x+h)^2 - 3 = 5x^2 + 10xh + 5h^2 - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and simplify.

\textit{Step 2: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(5x^2 + 10xh + 5h^2 - 3) - (5x^2 - 3)}{h} = \frac{10xh + 5h^2}{h} = 10x + 5h
\end{gathered}\]
Constants cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}(10x + 5h) = 10x \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
The \( 5h \) term vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown: \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 10x \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 47\quad\small\texttt{(d1-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 47 (d1-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Use first principles to find the gradient of the curve \( y = x^2 + 2x \) at the point where \( x = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = x^2 + 2x \). Form \( f(3+h) - f(3) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(3+h) = (3+h)^2 + 2(3+h) = 9 + 6h + h^2 + 6 + 2h = 15 + 8h + h^2
\end{gathered}\]
\( f(3) = 15 \).

\textit{Step 2: Subtract \( f(3) \) and divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{8h + h^2}{h} = 8 + h
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify the difference quotient.

\textit{Step 3: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(3) = \lim_{h \to 0}(8 + h) = 8
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient at \( x = 3 \) is 8.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 3 \) is 8.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 48\quad\small\texttt{(d1-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 48 (d1-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find, from first principles, the gradient of the curve \( y = x^3 \) at the point where \( x = -2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = x^3 \). Compute \( f(-2+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-2+h)^3 = -8 + 12h - 6h^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the cube.

\textit{Step 2: Subtract \( f(-2) = -8 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
12h - 6h^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
The \( -8 \) cancels.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(-2) = \lim_{h \to 0}(12 - 6h + h^2) = 12
\end{gathered}\]
The \( h \) terms vanish.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = -2 \) is 12.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 49\quad\small\texttt{(d1-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 49 (d1-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Using first principles, find the gradient of the curve \( y = 4x^2 - x \) at \( x = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = 4x^2 - x \). Then \( f(1) = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(1+h) = 4(1+h)^2 - (1+h) = 4 + 8h + 4h^2 - 1 - h = 3 + 7h + 4h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and simplify.

\textit{Step 2: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(3 + 7h + 4h^2) - 3}{h} = 7 + 4h
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel and divide.

\textit{Step 3: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(1) = \lim_{h \to 0}(7 + 4h) = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Limit as \( h \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \) is 7.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 50\quad\small\texttt{(d1-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 50 (d1-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = x^2 - 6x + 5 \). Use first principles to show that the gradient of the curve at \( x = 4 \) is 2.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = x^2 - 6x + 5 \). Compute \( f(4) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(4) = 16 - 24 + 5 = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 4 \).

\textit{Step 2: Compute \( f(4+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(4+h)^2 - 6(4+h) + 5 = 16 + 8h + h^2 - 24 - 6h + 5 = -3 + 2h + h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and simplify.

\textit{Step 3: Difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(-3 + 2h + h^2) - (-3)}{h} = 2 + h
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel and divide.

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(4) = \lim_{h \to 0}(2 + h) = 2 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
As required.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown: gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \) is 2.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 51\quad\small\texttt{(d1-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 51 (d1-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 2x^2 + 5x \). Find the gradient of \( C \) at the point where \( x = -1 \), using first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = 2x^2 + 5x \). Then \( f(-1) = 2 - 5 = -3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(-1 + h) = 2(-1+h)^2 + 5(-1+h)
\end{gathered}\]
Compute \( f(-1+h) \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2(1 - 2h + h^2) + (-5 + 5h) = 2 - 4h + 2h^2 - 5 + 5h = -3 + h + 2h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Collect like terms.

\textit{Step 3: Form the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(-3 + h + 2h^2) - (-3)}{h} = 1 + 2h
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( -3 \), divide by \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(-1) = \lim_{h \to 0}(1 + 2h) = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Limit as \( h \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = -1 \) is 1.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 52\quad\small\texttt{(d1-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 52 (d1-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find, from first principles, the gradient of \( y = x^3 + x \) at the point where \( x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( f(x) = x^3 + x \). Then \( f(2) = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(2+h) = (2+h)^3 + (2+h) = 8 + 12h + 6h^2 + h^3 + 2 + h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand cube and linear term.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 10 + 13h + 6h^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Combine constant terms.

\textit{Step 3: Difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{13h + 6h^2 + h^3}{h} = 13 + 6h + h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel and divide.

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(2) = \lim_{h \to 0}(13 + 6h + h^2) = 13
\end{gathered}\]
Limit as \( h \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \) is 13.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 53\quad\small\texttt{(d1-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 53 (d1-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = x^2 + 4x - 7 \). (a) Find \( \dfrac{dy}{dx} \) from first principles. (b) Hence find the gradient of the curve at the point where \( x = -3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Compute \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
[(x+h)^2 + 4(x+h) - 7] - [x^2 + 4x - 7] = 2xh + h^2 + 4h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and subtract.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = \lim_{h \to 0}(2x + h + 4) = 2x + 4
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient function.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Substitute \( x = -3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2(-3) + 4 = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate the derivative.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 2x + 4 \) \quad  (b) Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = -3 \) is \(\displaystyle  -2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 54\quad\small\texttt{(d1-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 54 (d1-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 - 5x \). (a) Use first principles to find the gradient function. (b) Find the value of \( x \) at which the gradient of \( C \) is zero.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(x+h)^2 - 5(x+h) - (x^2 - 5x)}{h} = \frac{2xh + h^2 - 5h}{h} = 2x + h - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Expand, subtract, divide.

\textit{Step 2: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 2x - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Set gradient \( = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 5 = 0 \Rightarrow x = \tfrac{5}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \). At this \( x \) the tangent is horizontal --- the curve has its minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 2x - 5 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = \tfrac{5}{2} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 55\quad\small\texttt{(d1-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 55 (d1-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = 3x^2 - 12x + 5 \). Using first principles, find the value of \( x \) at which the tangent to the curve is horizontal.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute the difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{3(x+h)^2 - 12(x+h) + 5 - (3x^2 - 12x + 5)}{h} = \frac{6xh + 3h^2 - 12h}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and simplify.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = \lim_{h \to 0}(6x + 3h - 12) = 6x - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function.

\textit{Step 3: Tangent horizontal when \( \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x - 12 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Horizontal tangent at \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 56\quad\small\texttt{(d1-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 56 (d1-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( C \) is given by \( y = x^3 - 3x \). (a) Find \( \dfrac{dy}{dx} \) from first principles. (b) Find the gradient of \( C \) at the points where \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Compute \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+h)^3 - 3(x+h) - x^3 + 3x = 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3 - 3h
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the cube.

\textit{Step 2: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = \lim_{h \to 0}(3x^2 + 3xh + h^2 - 3) = 3x^2 - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Substitute.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{x=0} = -3, \quad \left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{x=1} = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate at both values.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 3x^2 - 3 \) \quad  (b) Gradients are \(\displaystyle  -3 \) and \(\displaystyle  0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 57\quad\small\texttt{(d1-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 57 (d1-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 + 2x - 3 \). Find the coordinates of the point on \( C \) at which the gradient is equal to 6.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate (by first principles or directly).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 2x + 2
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function for a quadratic.

\textit{Step 2: Set gradient \( = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 2 = 6 \Rightarrow x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4 + 4 - 3 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Point is \(\displaystyle  (2, 5) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 58\quad\small\texttt{(d1-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 58 (d1-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the coordinates of the point on the curve \( y = x^2 - 4x + 1 \) where the gradient is \( -2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 2x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function.

\textit{Step 2: Set gradient equal to \( -2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 4 = -2 \Rightarrow x = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 1 - 4 + 1 = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Point is \(\displaystyle  (1, -2) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 59\quad\small\texttt{(d1-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 59 (d1-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The displacement \( s \) metres of a particle at time \( t \) seconds is modelled by \( s = t^2 + 3t \). (a) Find the velocity \( \dfrac{ds}{dt} \). (b) Find the velocity when \( t = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate \( s \) with respect to \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{ds}{dt} = 2t + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function --- the velocity.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Substitute \( t = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2(4) + 3 = 11
\end{gathered}\]
Units: m/s.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{ds}{dt} = 2t + 3 \) \quad  (b) Velocity is 11 m/s.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 60\quad\small\texttt{(d1-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 60 (d1-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A ball is thrown so that its height \( h \) metres after \( t \) seconds is \( h = 20t - 5t^2 \). Find the rate at which the height is changing when \( t = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate with respect to \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dh}{dt} = 20 - 10t
\end{gathered}\]
Rate of change of height.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
20 - 10(1) = 10
\end{gathered}\]
The ball is rising at 10 m/s. The diagram shows the height-time curve and the tangent at \( t = 1 \), whose gradient \( 10 \) is the instantaneous velocity.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Rate of change of height is 10 m/s (rising).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 61\quad\small\texttt{(d1-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 61 (d1-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The volume \( V \) cm\( ^3 \) of a balloon at time \( t \) seconds is \( V = 4t^2 + t \). Find \( \dfrac{dV}{dt} \) and state the rate at which the volume is increasing when \( t = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dV}{dt} = 8t + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Rate of change of volume.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
8(3) + 1 = 25
\end{gathered}\]
Units: cm\( ^3 \)/s.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dV}{dt} = 8t + 1 \); rate \(\displaystyle  = 25 \) cm\(\displaystyle  ^3 \)/s.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 62\quad\small\texttt{(d1-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 62 (d1-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A company models its profit \( P \) (in \pounds thousands) as a function of units sold \( x \) by \( P = -x^2 + 40x - 100 \). Find \( \dfrac{dP}{dx} \) and determine the value of \( x \) at which profit is momentarily constant (the marginal profit is zero).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dP}{dx} = -2x + 40
\end{gathered}\]
Marginal profit.

\textit{Step 2: Set equal to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2x + 40 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 20
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dP}{dx} = -2x + 40 \); profit is momentarily constant at \(\displaystyle  x = 20 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 63\quad\small\texttt{(d1-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 63 (d1-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A cup of hot coffee cools so that its temperature \( T \) \textdegree C after \( t \) minutes is modelled by \( T = 90 - 2t^2 \). Find the rate of change of temperature when \( t = 3 \) and interpret your answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dT}{dt} = -4t
\end{gathered}\]
Rate of change of temperature.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( t = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-4(3) = -12
\end{gathered}\]
Negative means the coffee is cooling.

\textit{Step 3: Interpret.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Cooling at } 12 \text{ \textdegree{}C per minute}
\end{gathered}\]
The temperature is decreasing at 12 \textdegree C/min at this instant.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Rate \(\displaystyle  = -12 \) \textdegree C/min --- the coffee is cooling at 12 \textdegree C/min.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 64\quad\small\texttt{(d1-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 64 (d1-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Differentiate \( f(x) = x^4 \) from first principles.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand \( (x+h)^4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+h)^4 = x^4 + 4x^3 h + 6x^2 h^2 + 4x h^3 + h^4
\end{gathered}\]
Binomial expansion.

\textit{Step 2: Form \( f(x+h) - f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x^3 h + 6x^2 h^2 + 4x h^3 + h^4
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^4 \) cancels.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x^3 + 6x^2 h + 4x h^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel one factor of \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit as \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 4x^3
\end{gathered}\]
All \( h \) terms vanish.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 4x^3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 65\quad\small\texttt{(d1-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 65 (d1-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 2x^2 + x \). (a) Use first principles to find \( \dfrac{dy}{dx} \). (b) Hence find the values of \( x \) at which the tangent to \( C \) has gradient 1.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+h)^3 - 2(x+h)^2 + (x+h)
\end{gathered}\]
Expand term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Expand and collect.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3 - 2x^2 - 4xh - 2h^2 + x + h
\end{gathered}\]
Binomial expansion of each bracket.

\textit{Step 3: Subtract \( f(x) = x^3 - 2x^2 + x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 h + 3xh^2 + h^3 - 4xh - 2h^2 + h
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel \( f(x) \) terms.

\textit{Step 4: Divide by \( h \) and take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = \lim_{h \to 0}(3x^2 + 3xh + h^2 - 4x - 2h + 1) = 3x^2 - 4x + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient function.

\textit{Step 5: (b) Set gradient \( = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 4x + 1 = 1 \Rightarrow 3x^2 - 4x = 0 \Rightarrow x(3x - 4) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise.

\textit{Step 6: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \text{ or } x = \tfrac{4}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Two solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 3x^2 - 4x + 1 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \) or \(\displaystyle  x = \tfrac{4}{3} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 66\quad\small\texttt{(d1-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 66 (d1-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Use first principles to show that if \( f(x) = ax^2 + bx + c \) then \( f'(x) = 2ax + b \), where \( a, b, c \) are constants.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute \( f(x+h) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
a(x+h)^2 + b(x+h) + c = ax^2 + 2axh + ah^2 + bx + bh + c
\end{gathered}\]
Expand.

\textit{Step 2: Subtract \( f(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2axh + ah^2 + bh
\end{gathered}\]
\( ax^2, bx, c \) cancel.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2axh + ah^2 + bh}{h} = 2ax + ah + b
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify.

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0}(2ax + ah + b) = 2ax + b \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
As required.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown: \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 2ax + b \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 67\quad\small\texttt{(d1-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 67 (d1-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The points \( A \) and \( B \) both lie on the curve \( y = x^2 \). The chord \( AB \) has gradient 5. Given that the \( x \)-coordinate of \( A \) is \( a \) and the \( x \)-coordinate of \( B \) is \( a + h \) with \( h \neq 0 \), show that \( 2a + h = 5 \). Hence state the gradient of the tangent to the curve at the midpoint of \( AB \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Gradient of \( AB \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(a+h)^2 - a^2}{(a+h) - a} = \frac{2ah + h^2}{h} = 2a + h
\end{gathered}\]
Standard chord-gradient calculation.

\textit{Step 2: Set equal to 5.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2a + h = 5 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
As required.

\textit{Step 3: Midpoint of \( AB \) has \( x \)-coordinate \( a + \tfrac{h}{2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x_M = a + \frac{h}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Average of the two \( x \)-coordinates.

\textit{Step 4: Gradient of tangent at midpoint is \( 2x_M \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{x_M} = 2\!\left(a + \frac{h}{2}\right) = 2a + h = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Using \( \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 2x \) for \( y = x^2 \). The diagram illustrates that the tangent at the midpoint of \( AB \) is parallel to (in fact has the same gradient as) the chord \( AB \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown \(\displaystyle  2a + h = 5 \). The tangent at the midpoint has gradient 5 (equal to the chord gradient).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 68\quad\small\texttt{(d1-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 68 (d1-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 - 2x + 4 \). The point \( P \) lies on \( C \) and has \( x \)-coordinate 3. (a) Find the coordinates of \( P \). (b) Use first principles to find the gradient of \( C \) at \( P \). (c) Find the equation of the tangent to \( C \) at \( P \), in the form \( y = mx + c \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 9 - 6 + 4 = 7
\end{gathered}\]
So \( P = (3, 7) \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Form \( f(3+h) - f(3) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3+h)^2 - 2(3+h) + 4 - 7 = 9 + 6h + h^2 - 6 - 2h + 4 - 7 = 4h + h^2
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify.

\textit{Step 3: Difference quotient.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{4h + h^2}{h} = 4 + h
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \( h \).

\textit{Step 4: Take the limit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(3) = \lim_{h \to 0}(4 + h) = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient at \( P \) is 4.

\textit{Step 5: (c) Tangent with gradient 4 through \( (3, 7) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y - 7 = 4(x - 3) \Rightarrow y = 4x - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Point-gradient form, then simplify. The diagram shows curve \( C \) and the tangent \( y = 4x - 5 \) touching at \( P \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  P = (3, 7) \) \quad  (b) Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 4 \) \quad  (c) \(\displaystyle  y = 4x - 5 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 69\quad\small\texttt{(d1-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 69 (d1-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 \). The chord joining the points \( P(p, p^2) \) and \( Q(q, q^2) \) has gradient \( m \), where \( p \neq q \). (a) Show that \( m = p + q \). (b) Hence, by letting \( q \to p \), deduce the gradient of the tangent to \( C \) at \( P \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Gradient of chord.}
\[\begin{gathered}
m = \frac{q^2 - p^2}{q - p}
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient formula.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the numerator.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{(q - p)(q + p)}{q - p} = q + p \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Difference of two squares; cancel \( q - p \neq 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: (b) Let \( q \to p \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\lim_{q \to p}(p + q) = 2p
\end{gathered}\]
Chord becomes tangent. In the diagram, as \( Q \) slides along the curve toward \( P \), the chord \( PQ \) rotates into the tangent at \( P \), whose gradient is \( 2p \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Shown \(\displaystyle  m = p + q \). \quad  (b) Tangent gradient at \(\displaystyle  P \) is \(\displaystyle  2p \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{The Gradient of a Curve 70\quad\small\texttt{(d1-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Gradient of a Curve 70 (d1-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = x^3 \). The chord joining the points \( (a, a^3) \) and \( (a + h, (a+h)^3) \), where \( h \neq 0 \), has gradient \( m \). (a) Show that \( m = 3a^2 + 3ah + h^2 \). (b) By letting \( h \to 0 \), deduce that the gradient of the tangent to the curve at \( x = a \) is \( 3a^2 \). (c) Hence find the gradient of the curve at the point where \( x = -1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Gradient of chord.}
\[\begin{gathered}
m = \frac{(a+h)^3 - a^3}{(a+h) - a} = \frac{(a+h)^3 - a^3}{h}
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient formula.

\textit{Step 2: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(a+h)^3 - a^3 = 3a^2 h + 3a h^2 + h^3
\end{gathered}\]
Binomial expansion.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( h \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
m = 3a^2 + 3ah + h^2 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
As required.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Let \( h \to 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\lim_{h \to 0}(3a^2 + 3ah + h^2) = 3a^2
\end{gathered}\]
All \( h \) terms vanish; chord becomes tangent.

\textit{Step 5: (c) Substitute \( a = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3(-1)^2 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient at \( x = -1 \) is 3. The diagram shows the curve \( y = x^3 \) and the tangent at \( (-1, -1) \) with gradient 3.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Shown. \quad  (b) Tangent gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = a \) is \(\displaystyle  3a^2 \). \quad  (c) Gradient at \(\displaystyle  x = -1 \) is 3.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}
